# Dispositions of the 73 reviewer comments

Branch `phase-e-review`, 21 Nov 2026. One entry per tracker ID, matched to the full wording in
`detailed review comments.txt`. Statuses:

- **Fixed**: all required changes complete and verified; any other request in the row has an evidence-backed rationale.
- **Partially fixed**: at least one substantive request resolved, another open.
- **Not fixed**: substantive work or verification remains and no substantive request is resolved.
- **No change warranted**: fully addressed by a documented rationale, a clarification-only answer or a verified guardrail.

The manuscript was not edited in this pass, so a request whose remedy is manuscript text stays open until the text
is applied (proposals in `notes/manuscript_proposals.md`, cited as P-A1 and so on). Shared work carries the same
status to every ID it serves. Figures and provenance: `notes/number_audit.md` (cited as NA-section). Commits are on
`phase-e-review` unless marked `main`. Pre-calibration means fast simulator, assumed constants, results to be
regenerated. The *Erlang-B relative error* and the *end-to-end integer-search saving* are both 1.92% and unrelated.

## R1.1 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) Elaborate on the likely size of SLA violations on AWS Lambda: open (needs the live run, R6.1). (b) Recommend an operator safety margin: partly. Resolved as a conditional simulation recommendation: a 10% margin on every stage's provisioned concurrency when the validation-selected eps <= 0.02, validated out of sample on prespecified fresh traces (20 of 20 weeks; 5 of 5 traces; +6.1% cost; open loop, pre-calibration). Open: the eps >= 0.03 branch (unvalidated: 2 of 30 further fresh traces select it, fewer than the prespecified 3), and a production margin (needs the calibrated closed-loop evaluation and a live check).

On current code the submitted configuration misses the weekly target in 8 of 20 held-out fast-simulator weeks (NA-6), so a margin is needed. The eps mapping (637596a) gives an operator rule within the simulator. Event-level margin counterfactual, 22 Nov 2026 (PRE-CALIBRATION, POST HOC, OPEN LOOP; see R2.4): on PRISM's Table XI schedules, a uniform margin on every stage's provisioned capacity meets 2% in 16 of 20 weeks at 10% (+6.1% cost) and 17 of 20 at 20% (+11.8%); the individual missed weeks needed 10-50%. These are counterfactual replays on assumed constants, not a margin for AWS Lambda. Resumed event-level replays, 22 Nov 2026 (PRE-CALIBRATION, POST HOC, OPEN LOOP: stored E1 schedules replayed through the current event-level code at HEAD 78ba419, src/ and experiments/ clean; seeds 101-105 only; the 187 saved design rows were reused unchanged and the 41 missing replays run in a separate folder, 12:53-13:04 UTC; all 228 design rows present exactly once; prism-repro/docs/revision/event_severity_resumed_20261122/summary.txt, README.md; the partial summary is kept in prism-repro/docs/revision/verification_20261122/): complete uniform-margin counterfactual on PRISM's Table XI schedules: 10% meets 2% in 16/20 weeks (+6.1% cost), 20% in 17/20 (+11.8%), 30% in 19/20 (+17.7%), 50% in 20/20 (+29.2%). Simulation on assumed constants, not a margin for AWS Lambda. 23 Nov 2026 (10-ID pass): (b) candidate margin rule, checked out of sample (prism-repro/docs/revision/margin_r11_20261123/: PLAN.md fixed before the analysis, margin_rule.py, result.txt; PRE-CALIBRATION, OPEN LOOP, POST HOC event-level replays of PRISM's E12-selected schedules, development traces 101-105, 20 weeks, margins 0-50%): rule = smallest uniform margin at which q of the training weeks meet 2%; leave one trace out. Unfavourable: with q = 1.0 and q = 0.9 alike, 16 of 20 held-out weeks meet 2% (95% Clopper-Pearson 56-94%); all four failures are trace 103 (2.08-2.83% at the 10% margin chosen from the other traces; it needs 20-50%); mean cost of the chosen margin +24.9% (q = 1.0) and +15.5% (q = 0.9). In sample, 10% meets 2% in 16 of 20 weeks and 50% in 20 of 20 (+29.2%). Reproduced: margin_rule.py gives a result byte-identical to margin_r11_20261123/result.txt (16 of 20 held-out weeks; all 4 failures trace 103 at 10%). Diagnosis supported by the stored logs (rows_R24, results/raw/E12): 103's event-level validation selected the largest risk budget (eps 0.03; others 0.005-0.02) with the least slack (1.935%, 0.07 points below 2%; others 0.21-0.51), its validation week was the easiest at every eps, its validation-to-test shift was the largest (+1.38 points; others -0.26 to +0.41), its overflow share the highest (1.10-1.17%; others 0.32-0.86%), and with instantaneous initialisation all 20 weeks show 0.00% violating windows. Open: (a) the size of violations on AWS Lambda (the event-level replays attribute every violation to initialisation occupancy, so it depends on the application's real start-up time, which memcal-1 neither tested nor contradicted; needs the live run, R6.1); (b) the eps >= 0.03 branch (needs a larger prespecified search or another design; it is rare under the tuning: 1 of 5 development and 2 of 30 fresh traces); (b) a production margin (calibrated closed-loop evaluation and a live check); the manuscript text (notes/manuscript_proposals.md M17). 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): status unchanged: pursue the 50% branch only if the paper keeps a general margin claim, with a new validation-only selection plan fixed before any test outcome, on unused traces; a production margin needs the calibrated constants and the live run. 24 Nov 2026 (calibration pass, no AWS call, no rerun): the production margin depends on P3 (start-up) above all: the event-level violations come from initialisation occupancy. 24 Nov 2026 (calibration decisions and bounded closed-loop check; no AWS call): the margin finding keeps its pre-calibration scope; P2's common-factor reading would make 1024 MB warm-infeasible at 14.5 s and must be settled before a calibrated margin check. P2 is unresolved and material: the simulator draws per-stage execution spread independently, while the docpipe document drives all four stages; with a common factor the warm miss probability at 1024 MB rises from 0.24% to 3.33% at 14.5 s and from 1.18% to 7.17% at 13 s (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_final_20261124/p2_correlation_check.txt).
- Suggested response: (a) Violations on AWS Lambda. We cannot yet state their size, and we say so. In the event-level replays every violating window comes from initialisation occupancy: with instantaneous initialisation, they show no violating window in any of the 20 development weeks. The magnitude on Lambda therefore depends mainly on the application's actual start-up time, which our AWS measurements of a prototype with no start-up work neither test nor contradict (the simulators assume 6 s). A paired live comparison of PRISM and an on-demand baseline on identical records is prepared and pending. (b) Safety margin. In the event-level simulator (open loop, pre-calibration constants), a single margin chosen on other traces did not transfer: it failed on the one development trace whose validation week selected the largest risk budget. We therefore made the margin depend on the budget the operator selects, and tested this rule on five fresh traces with the rule and criterion fixed in advance. When validation selects a budget of at most 0.02, adding 10% to every stage's provisioned concurrency met the 2% target in 20 of 20 weeks (all four weeks of all five traces; 95% interval for the trace-level rate 48-100%), against 16 of 20 without a margin, at 6.1% higher cost (5.8-6.5% over traces). We recommend this only as conditional guidance from simulation. For budgets of 0.03 or more we make no recommendation: only 2 of 30 further fresh traces selected such a budget, too few to test the larger margin. A margin for production on AWS Lambda needs the calibrated closed-loop evaluation and the live check. [Manuscript: pending.]

## R1.2 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) How sensitive are the results to the assumption that initialisation shortens with memory: resolved in simulation, pre-calibration. (b) Would memory co-optimisation still help if initialisation were constant or grew with memory: resolved in simulation, pre-calibration. (c) Ground the direction on measurements: partly (direction measured on AWS Lambda for the declared synthetic workload, memcal-1: Lambda Init Duration constant in memory; sweep not yet re-centred).
- Changed / rationale: init_scaling option eq3 / constant / inverted / measured (131f024); sweep in the regime where memory matters (a4187f7).
- Evidence: rho at 2048 MB is 0.094 under Eq. (3), 0.196 constant, 0.350 inverted (tests/test_analytic.py::TestInitScalingDirection). At a 12 s deadline PRISM moves to >= 2048 MB in every epoch under all three directions and stays compliant (0.00 / 0.07 / 0.30% violating windows), at 3.4% (constant) and 5.4% (inverted) higher cost; at 13 s eps = 0.02 lets PRISM stay at 1024 MB and ~96% of windows violate under every direction (docs/revision/sensitivity/summary.txt; two traces). External data, 21 Nov 2026 (exploratory, post hoc, historical external data; not a PRISM measurement; prism-repro/docs/revision/init_vs_memory/rerun_20261121-163247/RESULTS.md): three different quantities. (1) Lambda-reported initialisation (SeBS, AWS Lambda us-east-1, November 2020, six benchmarks, 8,165 cold invocations with a reported init duration) is nearly flat with memory: medians 0.01-0.19 s; log-log slopes -0.105 to +0.029 over 512-3008 MB against Eq. (3)'s -0.273. The large-package benchmark 411 is flat (+0.009, interval -0.009 to +0.023). Eq. (3)'s ratio lies inside the interval in 2 of 31 benchmark-memory cells, both day-confounded. The 503 decrease and the Node.js thumbnailer increase do not survive the same-day check. (2) The cold-minus-warm client-time penalty falls with memory in 5 of 6 benchmarks (slopes -0.29 to -0.68, at least as steep as Eq. (3)) and is inconclusive for the Node.js thumbnailer. (3) The {HUAWEI} decreases within the same function in Region 1 (15 functions, median slope -0.45, sign test p = 0.035). It is inconclusive in Region 2 (9 functions) and gives no within-function evidence in Regions 3-5. No AWS source shows initialisation growing with memory, including for the large-package benchmark. AWS measurement harness, 21 Nov 2026 (prepared and validated locally; not run on AWS; prism-repro/aws_measurement/README.md, prism-repro/aws_measurement/RUNBOOK.md, prism-repro/aws_measurement/validation/dry_run_20261121-172542/, prism-repro/docs/revision/init_vs_memory/CLOSURE_MAP.md): the harness keeps Lambda-reported initialisation (Init Duration) separate from the full first-call penalty (cold minus warm end-to-end client time), the first-invocation excess and the unreported remainder, at 512, 1024, 2048 and 3008 MB, and applies MEASUREMENT_PLAN.md's decision rule against Eq. (3). The stage handlers are labelled proxies, because PRISM's stages are specified only statistically. Two init-placement proxies put start-up work at module scope or in the first call. The full-plan dry run recorded 23,168 of 23,168 invocations (prism-repro/aws_measurement/validation/dry_run_20261121-172542/full/dry_run_check.json). The 33 harness tests and 124 project tests are defined in the saved test files and were observed passing on 21 Nov 2026; no test log was saved. No live measurement exists. memcal-1 multi-memory calibration campaign, 22 Nov 2026 (run AWS_memcal_20261122-110627, by the author; analysed from the saved files, no AWS call): us-east-2, on demand, docpipe-1 four-stage workload with K frozen and no start-up work (INIT_ROWS = 0), one reference document, 512/1024/2048/3008 MB, laptop client, at most 8 in flight; 20,992 invocations in three blocks plus a 256-invocation smoke run. Plan prespecified (prism-repro/aws_pipeline/MEMCAL_PLAN.md, committed 3bf7958 before any data). PRISM's adaptive controller did not run and no provisioned concurrency was configured. Sanitized aggregates and rebuild script: prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/ (RESULTS.md, build_output.txt, tables/, analyse_memcal_run.py, sources.sha256); a rebuild into a fresh directory reproduced all 16 generated files byte for byte. Coverage complete: 21,248 of 21,248 invocations matched to a CloudWatch REPORT by request ID, 0 missing, 0 differences from the log-tail copies; 0 errors, 0 throttles; 24 unmatched warm REPORTs of unknown origin excluded. (c) Lambda-reported Init Duration is flat in memory: median 113.7-116.0 ms in all 16 stage-size cells, slope of log I on log memory +0.001 to +0.008 per stage (Eq. (3): -0.273). The client-observed first-call penalty P (median cold minus median warm end-to-end) is 0.23-0.25 s and flat for ingest and transform, falls slightly for load (slope -0.031 [-0.057, -0.002]), and falls at Eq. (3)'s rate only for aggregate (-0.288 [-0.309, -0.269]), where the decline is cold-minus-warm Duration (CPU-bound first-call work), not initialisation; pooled P slope -0.116 [-0.131, -0.100], excluding -0.273. Prespecified decision on P (FIT, 2,000 batch-bootstrap replicates): ingest and transform 'constant (or measured)'; aggregate 'keep Eq. (3), refit'; load and pooled 'keep the direction, refit the non-scaling share'; P and I trends differ for aggregate, load and pooled. The Eq. (3) form fitted to I lands at the boundary init_nonscaling = 1.0 (constant). Calibration and validation: all fits use the FIT split (29 of 39 visits per stage and size, 15,592 invocations); the CHECK split (10 visits per cell, 4/3/3 over the blocks, 5,400 invocations), fixed in design.json before any data, is used only to test them: 37 of 56 predictions inside CHECK's 95% interval. Limits: the three blocks ran on one afternoon (11:18-13:51 CST), not on three days as planned, so day-to-day variation is unmeasured; the runtime version was not recorded; laptop stalls (5 warm batches) make the mean-based penalty P_mean not estimable for 3 of 4 stages; billing is billed GB-s at list price, not the bill. 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): calibration-input audit (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_inputs_20261124/README.md): each PipelineConfig() constant mapped to the saved AWS evidence. warm_mean_1024 matches memcal-1 by construction (frozen work units), so it is not independent evidence; the Eq. Event-level severity and factors, 22 Nov 2026 (PRE-CALIBRATION, POST HOC, OPEN LOOP: the stored E1 schedules at the stored E12 selections, replayed through the current event-level code at HEAD 78ba419; seeds 101-105; rows in prism-repro/docs/revision/event_severity/, uncommitted. Severity and persistence (OBSERVED): no two violating windows are consecutive in any of PRISM's 20 weeks (longest run 1); attainment in the worst window is 93.8-98.1% against the 99% requirement; the median shortfall of a violating window is 0.24-1.01 points. Workload conditions (ASSOCIATION): 249 of PRISM's 271 violating windows contain a random burst; windows with a burst violate at 24.7%, without at 0.18%; the top arrival-rate decile at 10.7%. Modelling factors (CAUSAL within the simulator): with initialisation instantaneous 20/20 weeks meet 2% (0.00% violating windows); with keep-alive unlimited 20/20 (0.31%); mean cost unchanged (USD 1,431-1,432). Extra provisioning (ESTIMATED by counterfactual replay, not a policy the controller could run without hindsight): the smallest uniform margin meeting 2% is 10% in three missed weeks (cost +5.8 to +6.6%), 20% in one (+10.7%), 30% in two (+16.2 to +16.5%) and 50% in one (trace 103 bursty, +28.0%); a uniform 10% margin meets 2% in 16/20 weeks at +6.1% cost, 20% in 17/20 at +11.8%. Incomplete: the 30% and 50% margins lack trace 105 new spike (19 of 20 weeks). The two missing margins for trace 105 new spike were run, and the earlier 'Incomplete' note is resolved. A uniform margin now gives: 10% meets 2% in 16/20 weeks (+6.1% cost); 20% in 17/20 (+11.8%); 30% in 19/20 (+17.7%); 50% in 20/20 (+29.2%; 0.54% violating windows).

It is open loop, pre-calibration and post hoc, so it must be rerun on the regenerated closed-loop, calibrated evaluation (R2.1, R2.2) before its figures enter the paper. - Suggested response: In the event-level retuning of Table XI, PRISM missed the target in 7 of 20 test weeks [figures to be regenerated]. The misses are mild and isolated rather than sustained: no two violating windows were consecutive in any week, the worst window still had 93.8-98.1% of records on time against the 99% requirement, and the median shortfall of a violating window was at most about one percentage point. They concentrate on one trace (trace 103, in all four scenarios) and in windows that contain random bursts (249 of 271 violating windows). In the simulator, making initialisation instantaneous, or keeping idle environments alive indefinitely, removes every miss, so cold starts during bursts are the modelling factor that produces them. Raising every stage's provisioned capacity uniformly by 10% would have met the target in 16 of 20 weeks at 6.1% more cost, and by 50% in all 20 at 29.2% more; the individual missed weeks needed 10-50%. These are pre-calibration, open-loop replays and are reported as such [pending: rerun on the closed-loop, calibrated evaluation].

## R2.5 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) State the measurable benefit of the optimizer beyond a calibrated upper-quantile rule with the same smoothing: resolved as a narrow, evidenced answer (manuscript text open). (b) Experiments in realistic regimes where the optimizer gives a substantial advantage: resolved as an end-to-end stated null, pre-calibration (no regime tested shows a consistent advantage).
- Changed / rationale: Static benchmarks of the candidate family (main c7360fe, 6cb3eef, 2bdeaba; pinned by bc548e7); level-choice provenance (e95fd8d); stage-specific demand in the fast simulator and the optimiser, guarded byte-identical at unit demand (85d909e, bde7d25); end-to-end runs (066f69c, docs/revision/stage_demand_e2e/).
- Evidence: End to end, 20 held-out weeks per cell, PRISM against QuantProv-q90 at the validation-selected settings: wider eps grid on the paper's workload -1.2 +/- 2.2% (matched compliance -1.0 to -2.3%); above break-even (smooth 6000/min) +11.1 +/- 0.8% (matched +9.0 to +9.9%); break-even 0.30 +4.3 +/- 1.5%, more compliant. Stage-specific demand: 3.1-6.8% cheaper than the phi-aware rule, but against a quantile rule with the same allocation shape -1.9 to -4.3% (filter), +3.3 to +5.4% (fan-out), -0.6 to -2.0% (batch) at matched compliance. Level choice: at most 0.11% of feasible epochs above the smallest for eps up to 0.05, 23.7% at 0.08 (common demand). Static restriction cost 2.71% (16 configurations); end-to-end integer-search saving 1.92% (Linux) with worse compliance in 4 of 6 runs (NA-4).
- Remaining: Blocker: author's framing decision (R4.1). Next: P-A1 to P-A4 with the corrected level-choice wording and the end-to-end null. Pass: text states the null and the conditions tested. The exploratory pilot driver was committed in 3e6021d. Original output unavailable in the supplied evidence; the pilot rerun falls outside the requested commit window.
- Suggested response: We tested the optimizer end to end where its reduction to a quantile rule should fail. The reduction does fail there (above the smallest feasible level in most epochs at larger eps, under fan-out and above break-even), but no regime shows a consistent cost advantage over a calibrated quantile rule with the same information: with a wider eps grid the difference is -1.2 +/- 2.2%, above break-even PRISM costs 9-11% more, and under stage-specific demand its 3-7% advantage over the phi-aware rule comes from its candidate family's per-stage allocation, which a quantile rule with the same allocation also captures. We state the benefit accordingly [pending the framing decision, R4.1].

- Suggested response: [pending D1] We added a memory-adaptive calibrated-quantile controller: it chooses the smallest memory size at which warm requests meet the deadline, from the same latency model PRISM uses, and provisions by the calibrated q0.9 rule. It selected the intended size for each tested regime (1024 MB at the default deadline, 2048 MB in every short-slack regime) [with independent per-stage execution spread; with the cross-stage dependence measured on AWS for our document pipeline, 1024 MB is not warm-feasible at the default deadline either, and the rule selects 2048 MB there too] and held that size for the run. In the fast-simulator regimes it meets the target as PRISM does, and at 12-13 s PRISM costs 8-28% more than it (it costs 8-22% less than PRISM) [under the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, which the AWS measurements contradict above 2048 MB; to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling; development check with measured per-size scaling: +11% at 13 s with independent stage spread and +21% with the measured shared spread; at the default deadline with shared spread, retuned, PRISM costs at least 17% more while both meet the target]. In the prespecified closed-loop comparison at 12 s (25 traces, assumed constants, run on Windows) PRISM costs 13% more but meets the target in more weeks (24 against 17 of 25), so there cost and compliance are not separated. We therefore narrowed the memory claim: memory selection is needed when the default size cannot meet the deadline, and a simple warm-feasibility rule achieves it.

- Suggested response: We added an exploratory experiment with a within-run change in execution characteristics: a step increase in every stage's execution time (CPU intensity) partway through the test week, in the fast simulator with the pre-calibration constants. An online-estimating PRISM variant, kept separate from the published configuration, was compared with one-time memory configurations; both received the same estimate of the new execution times one hour after the change. For the prespecified 50% increase, dynamic memory gave no benefit: the variant re-planned to 2048 MB, which could not meet the deadline target after the change, because its risk budget tolerates more warm misses than the window target allows, while a one-time choice of 3008 MB met the target at about 30% higher cost [this rests on the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, under which 3008 MB is faster than 2048 MB; the AWS measurements show no speed-up above 2048 MB, so the test is to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling]. For a smaller 25% increase, examined afterwards, dynamic memory met the target at 7-9% lower cost than the best one-time choice. So the benefit depends on the size of the change and on the risk budget relative to the target. Shifts in memory demand or initialisation latency were not tested, and the published configuration does not estimate execution times online; RQ4 remains narrowed to fixed regimes, where a one-time warm-feasible choice matched PRISM's compliance at 8-28% lower cost [under the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, which the AWS measurements contradict above 2048 MB; to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling]. [Manuscript: pending.]

PRISM assumes a 6 s mean initialisation at 1024 MB. The Huawei production provisioning delay (a different process) has function-weighted medians of 0.26-4.61 s at 1024 MB across five regions. - Suggested response: We measured warm execution times and initialisation on AWS Lambda for the four stage functions of a synthetic document pipeline, invoked individually on demand at 512, 1024, 2048 and 3008 MB: 20,992 invocations (1,312 per stage and size), each matched to Lambda's own REPORT record, with no errors or throttling. Median warm execution times at the four sizes were 1,624, 801, 463 and 463 ms for ingest; 8,039, 3,994, 2,292 and 2,287 ms for transform; 5,093, 2,529, 1,451 and 1,451 ms for aggregate; and 2,453, 1,205, 689 and 688 ms for load. Execution was thus 2.01-2.04 times faster at 1024 MB than at 512 MB, 1.73-1.75 times faster again at 2048 MB, and no faster at 3008 MB. Lambda's reported initialisation had a median of 113.7-116.0 ms in every stage and memory setting, for functions that do no application start-up work. The first-call penalty seen by the client (median cold minus median warm end-to-end time) was 226-258 ms for three stages at every size, and fell from 513 ms at 512 MB to 309 ms at 3008 MB for the aggregate stage, whose first call does extra CPU-bound work. The three measurement blocks were repeats on one afternoon: between blocks, median warm times agreed within 0.41% and median initialisation within 1.1-4.6 ms; day-to-day variation was not measured. Billed duration follows from the reported duration and initialisation, and the campaign's usage gives 55,234.5 GB-s for 20,992 requests (USD 0.92 at the public us-east-2 list price). AWS's estimated billing export for that region and day contains this usage: 21,784 requests and 57,005.6 GB-s in total, of which the calibration blocks are 20,992 and 55,234.5, and other saved runs that day most of the rest (prism-repro/docs/revision/billing_reconciliation_20261124/reconciliation.csv); the final charge is not yet available (see our response to comment 14). We fitted the latency forms on 29 of every 39 measurement visits and tested them on the other 10, chosen before any data were collected: 37 of 56 held-out predictions fell inside the held-out data's 95% batch-bootstrap interval. The single-term form of Eq. (2) missed at all 16 stage-memory settings (errors -10.1% to +13.1%) because execution stops speeding up above 2048 MB, and Eq. (3) fits Lambda's initialisation only as a constant (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/RESULTS.md). The client was a laptop; five warm batches had client-side stalls of up to 216.8 s, so the mean-based first-call penalty could not be estimated for three of the four stages, while the median-based results above are unaffected. Concurrency activation behaviour (provisioned concurrency) was not measured, the final charge on the provider's bill is not yet available, and the simulators have not yet been recalibrated on these values; these remain open. [Manuscript section and table: pending.]

## R2.9 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) Less structured traffic and irregular bursts: resolved in the fast simulator (EXPLORATORY, PRE-CALIBRATION, post hoc). All nine families ran on ten Huawei Public 2025 Region 2 traces, with the plan fixed in advance. (b) Unexpected periodicity changes: all families ran under the shifted-trigger scenario (fast simulator, exploratory; technical pass of 22 Nov 2026). (c) Different levels of temporal predictability: the ten traces span forecast median errors of 4.8-55.8%, with no association with PRISM's relative cost. All three remain open for the closed-loop, calibrated evaluation and the manuscript text.

Its plan, prism-repro/docs/revision/less_structured_r29_20261122/PLAN.md, was fixed and hashed before selection.
- Evidence: The stored Huawei replay has no trigger minutes assumed known; PRISM was still cheaper than static, scheduled and reactive there (results/tables/R1_real_traces.md), but costs and SLA outcomes are simulated. The generator's spike schedule (minutes 0, 1, 30) is given to the forecaster as features. Less-structured real traces, 22 Nov 2026 (prism-repro/docs/revision/less_structured_r29_20261122/ README.md, summary.txt, tables/per_trace.csv, selection_public.json, PLAN.md; EXPLORATORY, POST HOC, PRE-CALIBRATION, fast simulator, R1 protocol, simulator seeds 301-310). Traces: 10 of 11 eligible Region 2 functions (2,418 in the 28-day window), thinned to 600 per minute. They have no designed spikes or known trigger minutes; the 99.9th-percentile-to-median ratio is 2.3-39.4, one trace has 6,512 empty minutes, and the GBM test-week median APE is 4.8-55.8% (synthetic about 12%). Unfavourable first: PRISM meets 2% on 7 of 10 test weeks (pooled 1.37%) and misses on r2_00, r2_02 and r2_07; QuantProv-q90 also meets it on 7 of 10 and Static on 10 of 10. On the most irregular trace, Static meets the target at USD 255 and PRISM misses it (3.42%) at USD 563. Paired PRISM minus comparator, cost: QuantProv-q90 -6.5 +/- 11.3%, Reactive -7.4 +/- 17.2%, Static -0.5 +/- 33.9%, PredHead+S +13.8 +/- 36.7% (all include zero). Against Scheduled, -20.5 +/- 10.6%, but with +0.89 +/- 0.79 points more violating windows, so confounded by compliance. Spearman between forecast error and PRISM's cost relative to Reactive: -0.03 (p = 0.93). 55 of 80 selections sit at an edge of the paper's grid. Derived traces stay local (no licence stated for the source). 22 Nov 2026 check of the saved outputs: summary.txt prints the forecast median APE in percent (4.8-55.8%; the rows were never affected); its headline cost, violation and met-2% figures equal the per-trace table for all nine families; the 55 of 80 grid-edge selections are disclosed in summary.txt and the README. tables/per_trace.csv and selection_public.json are now written by the saved --analyse step (re-run on the saved rows only; the table reproduced byte for byte). selection.json (names the source funcIDs) and the derived traces stay local while the data licence is unresolved.
- Remaining: Blocker: author decision D5; scope. Next: plan #94-95 or the minimal version. Pass: all families on the new traces. 22 Nov 2026: notes/decisions_pending.md D5 updated (undecided). Existing inputs: the shifted_triggers scenario and the gbm_no_trigger forecaster in the code, and Huawei public 2025 Region 2 per-minute requests in additional data/ (the counts given in D5 were not re-checked here; no licence is stated, so derived traces should not be redistributed until the terms are checked). Smallest comparison proposed: five synthetic traces without the trigger features under shifted triggers, plus ten Region 2 functions chosen by a fixed rule, on GRIDS_WIDE; about 50 minutes of compute plus one to two hours of code. Not run. 22 Nov 2026: status Not fixed -> Partially fixed. The reviewer asks to 'evaluate less structured traffic patterns, irregular bursts, unexpected periodicity changes, and workloads with different levels of temporal predictability', and all families were evaluated on real traces with those properties under the paper's tuning rule (fast simulator; exploratory, pre-calibration). Open: the closed-loop, calibrated version; heavier-tailed synthetic bursts (D5 option a); the manuscript section and table (notes/manuscript_proposals.md I2); a licence check before redistributing derived traces.
- Suggested response: We evaluated every family on ten further production arrival traces, from the Huawei Public Cloud Trace 2025, Region 2. They have no designed spikes or known trigger minutes, include irregular bursts (a 99.9th percentile up to 39 times the median, and one trace with long idle periods), and span forecast errors from 5% to 56%. We used the same tuning rule and split as for the other real traces. PRISM met the 2% target on 7 of 10 traces, as did the calibrated q90 rule; static provisioning met it on all 10. No paired cost difference between PRISM and the calibrated-quantile, reactive, static or smoothed-headroom policies was distinguishable from zero, and PRISM's relative cost was not associated with forecast difficulty. We also evaluated every family under an unannounced change of the spike schedule. We therefore do not claim that the predictive cost advantage observed on the synthetic traces carries over to less structured traffic [figures to be regenerated].

## R2.11 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) Effect of update rate, clipping bounds, feedback delay and initialisation: resolved (sweep, pre-calibration). (b) Independent calibration per quantile level: resolved, including its cost-compliance effect. Manuscript text open.
- Changed / rationale: PrismConfig.conformal_per_quantile (4702440, default off); feedback delay floored by the lag (be7a912); PrismConfig.conformal_s0 and scripts/revision/calibration_sweep.py (38f0d67); sweep outputs (dc528db, docs/revision/calibration/).
- Evidence: Validation-selected, 20 held-out weeks: clipping never binds (wider bounds give identical runs); update rate and delay move cost -4.2% to +1.4% (weeks meeting 2%: 8-14 of 20); an initial offset of 0.4 makes selection less conservative (PRISM -4.9%, 8/20 weeks; QuantProv-q90 -5.8%, 7/20). Independent offsets: q0.9 coverage 98.0% -> 89.9% (stationary, NA-7); 6.8% / 5.7% cheaper at the selected settings but 6/20 weeks meet 2% (shared: 12/20, 11/20); at matched compliance -1.18% to +0.12% (post hoc). PRISM's selection is at eps 0.08, the grid edge, in 3-5 of 5 traces in every setting.
- Remaining: Blocker: none external. Next: prespecify the calibration (shared or per quantile) before the regeneration; rerun on the reference platform. Pass: text states the sweep and the matched-compliance result.
- Suggested response: The reviewer is right about the shared factor: with it the calibrated q0.9 covered 98% of outcomes in the stationary test weeks; with independent offsets it covers 90% and q0.99 is unchanged. Independent offsets do not improve the cost-compliance trade-off (-1.2% to +0.1% at matched compliance); at the selected settings they are cheaper but meet the target in fewer weeks. Clipping never binds, update rate and feedback delay move cost by -4.2% to +1.4%, and the initial offset matters through validation, where the submitted zero start makes selection conservative (see R4.6).

Earlier fast-only lower-floor cost penalty (+2.8–3.7% at matched compliance) remains historical evidence (8d8a816). Kappa evidence verified 21 Nov 2026 against prism-repro/docs/revision/kappa_gap/ rows. The event level replays the fast simulator's own schedule, so every decision is shared. Two quantities, reported separately. (1) Cold-start rate ratio: event-level over fast cold starts per on-demand request (whole week, four stages summed) is 3.23x, the mean of the per-trace ratios (range 2.97-3.59x; 0.0686 against 0.0213). Over stage executions the cold-start ratio is 0.00057 against 0.00020. (2) Kappa-estimate gap (the EWMA of per-epoch cold starts per on-demand request that the 0.1 floor clips): clipped 0.330 against 0.110 on the same schedule (3.00x, 2.40-3.72x) and 0.439 in closed loop (3.99x); unclipped 0.330 against 0.050 (6.98x, 4.34-9.59x) and 9.34x in closed loop (6.05-12.72x). The ratios were derived in prism-repro/docs/revision/closed_loop_pilot/verification.txt. The earlier 20-35x kappa-estimate gap appears in no saved file (PRIOR_EVIDENCE.md was not found). It is unverified and not used here. In the closed-loop pilot (floor 0.1) PRISM's test-week kappa was 0.384-0.442. Carried from notes/dispositions.md (22 Nov 2026 audit (window 21 Nov 2026 11:00 AM CST to this pass); historical fast-simulator comparison, 8d8a816, whose conclusion is on hold, c6ebc86): lower floor 0.001 against 0.1, mean kappa 0.06 instead of 0.10; cost +2.8 to +3.7% at matched compliance (post hoc) and +12.3% with eps selected on validation, target met in 20/20 against 12/20 held-out weeks; the earlier fixed-eps 13-21% saving is superseded (NA-6). 24 Nov 2026 (closed-loop follow-up pass, no AWS call): closed-loop kappa study (prism-repro/docs/revision/closed_loop_kappa_20261124/README.md; PLAN.md fixed before the run; Linux, clean clone of 3edc386; 15 of 15 tuned groups, 153 weeks, 0 failures, identical request streams). Arms: A floor 0.1 (default), B floor 0.001, F kappa frozen at 0.1. (2) B at A's eps (4 traces): P differs in 0.63% of epochs on average (max 7.7%), memory identical, cost -0.07 +/- 0.22% (stationary) else 0.00, violating windows +0.04 +/- 0.12 points or 0.00: the floor is inactive by the prespecified criterion. (3) F at A's eps (2-3 traces): P differs in 96-99.7% of epochs, 24-54 fewer provisioned units, cost -7.3 to -9.5%, violating windows +0.50 to +0.99 points. (4) Retuned, paired arm minus A, 5 traces: B +3.1 to +4.9% cost (all intervals include 0), violating windows -0.09 to -0.36 points; F -2.2 to -2.5% (all include 0), +0.00 to +0.30 points. Sensitivity weeks: 26 of 40 (the job was stopped by host memory pressure; 14 missing, listed in the README; resumable).
- Remaining: Retune and compare floors in closed loop before drawing a conclusion or using B3 in the response letter. 21 Nov 2026: the R2.12 floor comparison stays open. The pilot ran only the 0.1 floor, so floor 0.1 against 0.001 has not been retuned in closed loop. Proposal B3 stays on hold until that comparison exists. 24 Nov 2026 (closed-loop follow-up pass, no AWS call): status unchanged (Partially fixed): (a) and (b) are answered in simulation; open: the manuscript text (B3 with M20) and, optionally, the 14 missing sensitivity weeks (traces 104 and 105; resumable with run_linux.sh after the author's go-ahead, given host memory).
- Suggested response: Pre-calibration, exploratory. We reassessed the estimator under closed-loop event-level evaluation, where it responds to simulated telemetry. The observation that it stays at its 0.1 floor holds in the fast simulator, not there: at the risk budgets our tuning selects, the estimate averages about 0.42 and is at the floor in about 0.2% of test epochs, because event-level cold starts per overflow request are several times those of the fast simulator; it reaches the floor only at large budgets (up to 62% of epochs at eps = 0.08). So that operating regime is one in which the estimator remains active. Lowering the floor to 0.001 then changes almost no decision and no outcome. Freezing the estimate at 0.1 changes almost every decision and, at a fixed budget, lowers cost by 7-9% with 0.5-1.0 more points of violating windows; after the budget is retuned by our common rule, neither freezing it nor lowering the floor changes cost distinguishably on five development traces. The estimator therefore sets the operating point, but its effect on the tuned cost-compliance outcome is absorbed by the tuning; we state that it is not shown to be necessary under the default configuration. [Manuscript text: pending.]

Wording: manuscript_proposals.md L1. 24 Nov 2026 (local pass, no AWS; EXPLORATORY): status unchanged (Not fixed): (a) is partly met; provisioning initialisation and recycling need AWS's rule or a provisioned-concurrency billing measurement, and the components are not yet applied to the simulator's costs (a src/ change and rerun). (b) stays pending: no usable billing export is available locally. 24 Nov 2026 (status review): Not fixed -> Partially fixed. Resolved substantive parts of (a): billing granularity and the billing side of activation timing, both verified. Open in (a): initialisation and recycling of provisioned environments (AWS states no amount or rate; a provisioned-concurrency billing measurement would supply it); the activation-lead bound for QuantProv, PredHead and Reactive and its effect on C1-C3; a measured activation time (R6.1) in place of the one-epoch assumption. (b) open: no usable billing export. 24 Nov 2026 (billing reconciliation, no AWS call): supersedes the earlier notes that billing line items are unavailable: line items exist in the estimated export of 24 Nov 2026. Status unchanged (Partially fixed): (b) is partly met, usage verified but not charges. Still needed for (b): the final, non-estimated billing data; the Cost and Usage Report (pending case) with resource-level lines to attribute charges to the campaign's functions and explain the USD 0; attribution of the 80 requests and 838.43 GB-s. The billing-model work in (a) is tracked separately: initialisation and recycling of provisioned environments, and the activation-lead bound for the comparators. 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): status unchanged (Partially fixed): (a) open only for U1 and U2 (no AWS rule) and the C1-C3 schedules (not saved per epoch; a rerun saving them would be needed); a measured activation time from the R6.1 live run would replace the one-epoch assumption. (b) unchanged. The finding bears on the savings over Static and Scheduled cited in the abstract (R5.M7(a)). 24 Nov 2026 (closed-loop follow-up pass, no AWS call): status unchanged: (a) open for U1, U2, U4 and the C1-C3 schedules; (b) final charges and campaign-level attribution open (the estimated regional export's USD 0 is not attributed to the calibration blocks or to any credit).
- Suggested response: We computed the measurement campaign's cost from AWS Lambda's own usage records: 55,234.5 billed GB-s and 20,992 requests in the three calibration blocks, USD 0.92 at the public us-east-2 list prices (duration and requests only; free tier not assumed). Lambda billed each invocation's reported duration plus its initialisation, rounded up to the next millisecond, which our billing code now implements and tests; applied to the main comparisons it changes weekly costs by at most 0.09% and paired cost differences by at most 0.011 percentage points. We also implemented the provisioned-concurrency charges for which AWS states the rule (capacity billed from enabling, rounded up to five minutes; invocations on provisioned environments; overflow at on-demand rates), and checked them against AWS's worked pricing examples. On PRISM's schedules, the simulator's capacity charges equal AWS's rule exactly, because capacity changes only at five-minute boundaries. Activation time is a different matter: provisioned capacity takes a minute or two to come online, and if each increase is enabled one five-minute epoch early so that it is ready in time, PRISM's weekly cost rises by about 5-7% (6.2-7.4% for the configuration in our first check), more than the 1-2% differences at issue. Applied to every policy's schedules, the bound leaves the comparison with the calibrated quantile rule essentially unchanged (0.1-0.3 points) but reduces PRISM's savings over static and scheduled provisioning by 4-5 points, because those schedules rarely increase capacity; three of those savings intervals then include zero [values: development traces, fast simulator, pre-calibration]. AWS does not state the charge for initialising or recycling provisioned environments, so these remain unmodelled. We also compared our usage-based calculation with AWS's own billing export, provisionally. For us-east-2 on 22 November 2026 (UTC), the estimated export reports 21,784 Lambda requests and 57,005.6 GB-s; our saved records account for 21,372 of the requests and 56,167.1 of the GB-s, including the calibration blocks' 20,992 requests and 55,234.5 GB-s, and 332 further requests by saved request IDs, leaving 80 requests and 838.4 GB-s unattributed (aggregate table: prism-repro/docs/revision/billing_reconciliation_20261124/reconciliation.csv). The export reports no Lambda charge for the day and does not state why; it is estimated, so final charges and their attribution to our runs remain to be confirmed from the final bill [pending]. [Manuscript: pending.]

In the rehearsal it logged all 3 scheduled memory changes; their durations are fake-client timings, not AWS measurements. Provisioned-concurrency requests are logged, not applied (quota 10). Live memory reconfiguration, 21 Nov 2026 (live fixed-schedule integration test on AWS, 21 Nov 2026, us-east-2, 8:30-8:55 PM; the proposed synthetic document-ingestion workload, not the authors' application; not driven by PRISM's adaptive controller; separate from the manual Hello World Lambda test and from the local rehearsal of ea32370; AWS Live Implementation Log/AWS_livetest_20261121-203051/adapter_schedule/decisions.jsonl, AWS Live Implementation Log/AWS_livetest_20261121-203051/adapter_schedule/records.jsonl; audit of 21 Nov 2026 from the saved run files (session report; the audit is not saved as a file)): three memory changes of all four functions were applied live. Measured update duration (four UpdateFunctionConfiguration requests until all four functions reported the update successful, polled every 1 s): 3.27 s (1024 to 512 MB), 3.33 s (512 to 2048 MB) and 3.31 s (2048 to 1024 MB). Because the adapter starts each change at the epoch boundary, the new size took effect 3.26 s, 3.32 s and 3.31 s after that boundary, well before the next one 60 s later. The stage records show the first two changes in the memory size each stage reported; no record ran after the third. Also from the saved records: Step Functions stage-to-stage gaps, median 23-30 ms per transition (Lambda host clocks); client overhead (end-to-end minus service time), median 0.09-0.11 s. Decision times are those of a fixed schedule (microseconds), not PRISM's. The raw run files are private and untracked. 22 Nov 2026 (billing and usage audit): memcal-1 (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/tables/config_updates.csv; rebuilt from the private run with analyse_memcal_run.py, 15 earlier tables unchanged). Each of the 624 visits began with one UpdateFunctionConfiguration call that changed only an environment variable (to force cold starts; memory never changed). The client then polled GetFunctionConfiguration every 1 s until LastUpdateStatus was Successful. All 624 completed in 1.53-2.20 s (median 1.59 s, p95 1.89 s), with no difference by block or memory size. These are upper bounds at about 1 s resolution, including client API round trips; they are not memory-change times.
- Remaining: Blocker: AWS deployment. Next: plan #114-115. Pass: measured overheads against the 5-minute interval. Quota 10 measurement plan (8786295, proposal G; b9d84bd). Orchestration overhead can be measured from Step Functions histories. This is a measurement plan. 21 Nov 2026: live deployment, and measurement of the memory-reconfiguration delay, Step Functions orchestration overhead, telemetry collection and API-call delays against the 5-minute interval, are pending (prism-repro/aws_pipeline/LIVE_TEST.md). The provisioned-concurrency activation delay stays blocked under the quota of 10. 21 Nov 2026 (live run audit): open: provisioned-concurrency activation delay (none requested or applied), telemetry collection from CloudWatch (this run's telemetry came from client records), forecasting integration, and PRISM's own decision path running live. The memory-update timings come from one run with 1-s polling. 22 Nov 2026: notes/decisions_pending.md D7 (new, undecided): the live run's preflight read ConcurrentExecutions = 400 in us-east-2 (one reading), so provisioned concurrency may be configurable there; the 'quota of 10' blocker above may hold for us-east-1 only. Before any plan: a fresh read-only quota check, the adapter's pc_mode=apply (it raises NotImplementedError; versions and aliases not built) and separate authorisation of AWS use. The provisioned-concurrency activation delay would become measurable. 22 Nov 2026 (billing and usage audit): status unchanged. API-call latency for configuration updates is now measured (624 calls); telemetry collection, forecasting integration, provisioned-concurrency activation and PRISM's live decision path remain open.
- Suggested response: We ran a small cloud deployment on AWS Lambda and Step Functions, applying a fixed memory schedule through the same interface the controller uses. Each change of the shared memory size across the four functions completed in 3.27-3.33 s (three changes), about 1.1% of the five-minute decision interval; because each change starts at the epoch boundary, the new size took effect about 3.3 s into its epoch. The median gap between one stage finishing and the next starting, which includes the Step Functions transition, was 23-30 ms, and about 250 ms when the next stage started cold (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_live_integration/RESULTS.md; tables/memory_updates.csv, tables/overhead.csv). In a separate campaign of 624 configuration updates (UpdateFunctionConfiguration, changing an environment variable), every update was reported complete within 1.53-2.20 s (median 1.59 s) when polled once a second from the client, at every memory size (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/tables/config_updates.csv). Provisioned-concurrency activation delay, telemetry collection from the monitoring service, forecasting integration and PRISM's own decision path running live have not yet been measured; these remain open, and we say so. [Manuscript section: pending.]

- Suggested response: We ran the check on AWS Lambda with the four stage functions of a synthetic pipeline, invoked individually on demand at 512, 1024, 2048 and 3008 MB (20,992 invocations, each matched to Lambda's REPORT record). Lambda's reported Init Duration was approximately 115 ms at every tested memory setting (median 113.7-116.0 ms in all 16 stage-memory settings; slope of log initialisation on log memory +0.001 to +0.008 per stage, against -0.273 for Eq. (3)). It therefore showed neither the shortening assumed by Eq. (3) nor the lengthening reported in reference [8]. Our functions import only standard-library modules and do no application start-up work, so the run does not exercise package loading, which our threats-to-validity discussion cites as a reason larger allocations may start more slowly [section: pending]. Only the aggregate stage showed Eq. (3)'s memory trend in the first-call penalty observed by the client (slope -0.288, 95% interval -0.309 to -0.269), and that drop came from first-call execution: the cold invocation's own execution exceeded the warm one by 286 ms at 512 MB and 80 ms at 3008 MB, while initialisation stayed flat. For the other three stages the client-observed penalty stayed at 226-258 ms at every size. Pooled over the four stages, the penalty fell at -0.116 (95% interval -0.131 to -0.100), less than half of Eq. (3)'s rate. By the rule we fixed before the run, Eq. (3)'s direction is kept for the full first-call penalty with its non-scaling share refitted, and Eq. (3) does not describe Lambda's reported initialisation, which is constant in memory (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/RESULTS.md). The three blocks were repeats on one afternoon (median initialisation within 1.1-4.6 ms between blocks). The client was a laptop; five warm batches had client-side stalls of up to 216.8 s, so the mean-based first-call penalty could not be estimated for three of the four stages, while the median-based results above are unaffected. The magnitude of the modelled initialisation is not tested. The 2-minute activation lag remains open: it needs provisioned concurrency, which this run did not use. [Revised statement of Eq. (3) and its manuscript section: pending.]

- Changed / rationale: Not built. notes/decisions_pending.md D2 (5d934d4) sets out the scope problem: the hybrid histogram policy sets keep-alive and pre-warm windows for intermittently invoked functions, and at the paper's load no function is idle for a minute, so a faithful version has no published characteristic to reproduce here. Options and costs there.
- Evidence: Mean 600 executions per minute per stage (Table S1); none of the synthetic traces leaves a stage idle for a minute. Source and applicability check, 22 Nov 2026 (prism-repro/docs/revision/hybrid_histogram_r32_20261122/README.md, hybrid_windows.txt; scripts/revision/hybrid_histogram_check.py). The cited method was verified against the public USENIX ATC 2020 PDF (SHA-256 d6f71e84..., not stored). It sets per-application pre-warming and keep-alive windows (1-minute bins, 4-hour range, 5th and 99th percentiles, 10% margin, CV threshold 2, ARIMA beyond the range). Its authors exclude load-spike cold starts that need new instances ('we do not consider it in this paper'), and it reduces the provider's idle memory ('FaaS users do not pay for resources they do not use'). Its published result (Figure 15) uses the Azure Functions 2019 trace with execution times set to 0. On all 40 workloads studied (25 synthetic development traces, 5 R1 traces, 10 Region 2 traces), the policy gives pre-warm 0 and keep-alive 1.1 minutes and decides nothing about concurrent instances; it never unloads the application on the 35 traces without an idle minute, and on the sparse trace r2_00 application cold starts occur (minute-resolution approximation; EXPLORATORY; corrected 23 Nov 2026: this said it never unloads the application on all 40). 23 Nov 2026 (10-ID pass): the applicability analysis reproduces: scripts/revision/hybrid_histogram_check.py rerun into a fresh directory gives hybrid_windows.txt byte-identical to prism-repro/docs/revision/hybrid_histogram_r32_20261122/; on all 40 workloads the policy sets pre-warm 0 and keep-alive 1.1 min (longest idle gap 240 min on one Huawei 2025 trace, at most 3 min elsewhere), so it keeps every stage loaded and makes no concurrency decision. 23 Nov 2026 (R2.7/R3.2 review): prism-repro/docs/revision/r27_r32_review_20261123/NOTE.md: on the 40 evaluated traces (hybrid_histogram_r32_20261122/hybrid_windows.txt, reproduced byte for byte on 23 Nov 2026) the policy never unloads the application on 35 (no idle minute; pre-warm 0, keep-alive 1.1 min), finds idle gaps of at most 2-3 min and 0.0000% application cold starts at the reported precision on 4 Region 2 traces, and on the sparse trace r2_00 (6,512 empty minutes, gaps up to 240 min) unloads sooner than a fixed 10-min keep-alive (0.0049% against 0.0005% application cold starts). It decides nothing about concurrent instances on any trace; the source excludes load-spike cold starts. On every trace it leaves instance scaling to the platform; Table V's On-demand row, which does the same, is its nearest counterpart, but the two were not compared directly (corrected 23 Nov 2026: this said it reduces to on-demand execution with the application always loaded, i.e. Table V's On-demand row). Identity of [3]: no bibliography or manuscript file exists locally, so it is not confirmed; the policy named is that of Shahrad et al., USENIX ATC 2020 (checked against the source on 22 Nov 2026). 24 Nov 2026 (R3.2 reproduction attempt, local, no AWS; EXPLORATORY): prism-repro/docs/revision/hybrid_reproduction_r32_20261124/README.md (PLAN.md fixed before any run at 03:13:33 UTC, SHA-256 26d3f5e5..., unchanged). Source read: Shahrad et al., USENIX ATC 2020, Sections 4.2, 5.1, 5.2 (published PDF, kept privately). Data: Azure Functions 2019 trace from its official GitHub release (142,968,140 bytes, SHA-256 aff8b3ca...; 'CC-BY Attribution License'; citation of the ATC'20 paper requested), kept under prism-repro/private/, not committed. Implementation: scripts/revision/hybrid_policy.py (1-minute bins, 4-hour range, 5th/99th percentiles with the published rounding, 10% margin, CV threshold 2, standard keep-alive = range; ARIMA not implemented because its trigger is unspecified), 10 of 10 hand-calculated tests. Result on days 1-7 (21,546 applications; paper's simulator: execution time 0, equal memory): FAILED the prespecified criteria under both readings of the keep-alive window. Fixed keep-alive reproduced (75th-percentile app cold starts 52.60% at 10 min against 50.3% published; 25.00% at 1 h against 25%; 2-h wasted memory 1.299 x the 10-min policy against 'almost 30% higher'); no-unloading always-cold applications 2.84% against 3.5% (fail); 4-h hybrid: cold starts 25.0% (2.10 times fewer than fixed 10-min, published 2.5x) at 0.877 of the fixed 10-min wasted memory, where the paper reports the same memory (fail); always-cold without ARIMA 10.29% against 10.5%. A post hoc active-minute diagnostic fits worse. Blocker: two unspecified details (the ARIMA trigger and model input; the conversion of the minute-level trace to invocations and the application set) and no public implementation on the dataset page. Decision check on the 25 Table V test weeks (tablev_decisions/tablev_decisions.txt; not a Table V comparison): every stage is invoked in every minute (>= 98 records per minute, largest idle time 1 min), so the policy keeps every stage loaded throughout (pre-warm 0, loaded until 1.1 min after each invocation), as the platform keep-alive of 10 min assumed by the On-demand row does; image decisions identical on all 25 weeks; it sets no instance count. This holds for any keep-alive of at least one 1-minute bin, so it does not depend on the magnitudes that failed to reproduce. Reference [3] still unconfirmed (no manuscript or bibliography in the repository). 24 Nov 2026 (seven-ID pass, no AWS call): source search (prism-repro/docs/revision/r32_blocker_20261124/NOTE.md): no bibliography in the working tree, review inputs or any Git ref, so [3] remains unconfirmed. The paper (ATC 2020) releases 'a subset of our traces', notes that minute binning 'does not allow the precise reconstruction of interarrival times that are smaller than one minute', and specifies neither the ARIMA trigger ('too many OOB ITs') nor how its simulator turned per-minute counts into invocations; its simulations used the full workload. The official dataset pages call the public trace a per-application sample at a 'small and unspecified' rate, show it is representative for the characterisation figures, and do not address Figures 14-15 or the policy. A per-environment keep-alive variant would be our extension, not the cited method. Status unchanged; the comparison stays open. Smallest faithful test for the row (not run; prism-repro/docs/revision/r27_r32_review_20261123/NOTE.md): reproduce the published behaviour first on the first week of the Azure Functions 2019 trace (execution time 0, equal memory) and pass only if it gives about 2.5 times fewer application cold starts at the 75th percentile than a 10-min keep-alive at equal wasted memory (Figure 15); then apply the same implementation unchanged per stage on Table V's traces, weeks and simulator, as an exploratory row whose expected result is stated in advance. The Azure trace is not in the repository. 23 Nov 2026 (R6.1 pass, wording): the reply, the evidence, notes/manuscript_proposals.md M11, r27_r32_review_20261123/NOTE.md, notes/decisions_pending.md and hybrid_histogram_r32_20261122/README.md no longer say that the application stays loaded on every trace (the sparse trace r2_00 has application cold starts, 0.0049% against 0.0005% under a fixed 10-min keep-alive) or that the policy is equivalent to Table V's On-demand row (not compared directly). The finding that it decides no concurrent-instance count stands. Status unchanged. 24 Nov 2026 (R3.2 reproduction attempt, local, no AWS; EXPLORATORY): status unchanged (Not fixed): the single subrequest, a published controller in Table V, is not met, because the reproduction did not pass and no row was produced. Not supported: any claim that the policy was reproduced; comparisons on traces with idle gaps (for example r2_00); concurrency-control comparisons. 24 Nov 2026 (seven-ID pass, no AWS call): status unchanged (Not fixed): faithful reproduction is blocked by three unavailable inputs (the ARIMA trigger and its input series; the minute-level simulator convention; the full workload behind Figures 14-15). Open: the author's confirmation of [3]; whether to send the drafted inquiry to the policy's authors (NOTE.md section 6, not sent); the choice between the scope statement (M11, M14) and a row reported as an unreproduced deviation. No claim of end-to-end cost or SLA equivalence with the On-demand row is supported. 24 Nov 2026 (second seven-ID pass, no AWS call): still blocked; the inquiry to the policy's authors is final (NOTE.md section 6: the trigger and fitted series, the minute conversion and application set, the workload behind Figures 14-15, and the implementation or exact method) and NOT SENT; sending it is the author's decision. The 25-week application-loading decision check stays available for the reply, without any cost or SLA equivalence claim. 24 Nov 2026 (R3.2-only pass, no AWS call): Not fixed -> Partially fixed: the comparison with a published controller is verified; open: (1) the author chooses the row (recommended: OptScaler at published parameters, footnoted that the tuned version diverged) and the reporting platform; regenerate the row in that result set and insert it in Table V, with the paired values in Table VI; (2) a supplement section with the mapping, checks and the invalid tuned row; (3) the Section II scope statement for the hybrid histogram policy (M11, M14); (4) whether to send the unsent inquiry to the hybrid policy's authors. Pass: the row is in Table V from the reporting result set, with its footnote and supplement section. 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): status unchanged (Partially fixed): the Linux row is ready if Linux is the reporting basis (TABLE_V_ROW.md); insertion, the supplement section and the scope statement remain manuscript work.
- Suggested response: We tried both routes. The hybrid histogram policy of [3] was implemented as specified and tested against its published behaviour on the public Azure Functions 2019 trace, with criteria fixed in advance. It reproduced five of seven checks, but not the always-cold share without unloading (2.8% against 3.5%) nor the memory comparison. The ARIMA trigger, the minute-level conversion and the full workload are not published, so we do not report it as a reproduced baseline; it also decides whether an application is loaded, not how many instances run, and on every Table V week it keeps every stage loaded, as our On-demand row already assumes. We therefore added OptScaler [16], a published chance-constrained capacity controller evaluated on the Azure Functions trace, implemented from its paper with the same forecasts, traces, prices and SLA metric as every Table V row (mapping, tests and commands in the supplement). At its published target it met the 2% target on every trace and scenario, at 30-40% higher cost than PRISM (PRISM cost 23-28% less) with 1.2-1.9 points fewer violating windows (paired 95% intervals exclude zero), so the two are not at the same compliance and this is a trade-off, not evidence that either is better. Tuned by our common rule, its online estimator diverged, so we report no tuned row and say so. These are fast-simulator results on five development traces, before calibration. [Row, supplement section and Section II scope statement: pending; figures to be regenerated in the reporting result set.]

- Suggested response: Direct answer: we will narrow RQ4 rather than claim a broader test; the evidence supports only the narrowed question, and joint memory optimization shows no cost benefit over a simple rule in the regimes tested. (1) What PRISM decides. PRISM selects one memory size shared by all four stages, re-decided hourly (every 12 five-minute decision epochs; provisioned concurrency is decided every epoch), with execution characteristics (execution time, CPU intensity, memory demand, initialization latency) held fixed within each run. Per-stage memory sizes were not tested. (2) Load-driven changes versus adaptation. Within those fixed regimes PRISM could still change the shared size in response to load: at a 13 s deadline its validation-selected setting used both 2048 and 3008 MB in four of five test weeks (1.8 changes per week). These are responses to load, not adaptation to a shift in execution or initialization time: neither PRISM nor the baselines estimate those times online, so such adaptation is not part of the RQ4 claim. (3) Fixed-regime evidence (exploratory; fast simulator; five development traces; stationary weeks; pre-calibration). Where 1024 MB cannot meet the deadline (12-13 s), a memory-adaptive baseline that chooses the smallest warm-feasible size once per regime (2048 MB, no changes within the run) and PRISM (2048 MB or more) both met the 2% target in five of five test weeks with identical violating-window rates (0.00% at 13 s and at 12 s with non-scaling fraction 0.15; 0.03% at 12 s with 0.5), and PRISM cost more: +27.9%, +19.8% and +8.3% (mean of per-trace differences, relative to the baseline) [under the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, which the AWS measurements contradict above 2048 MB; to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling]. The baseline's setting was at the bottom of its grid, so its cost is an upper bound. At 11 s and 14.5 s the two achieved different compliance, so cost alone is not compared there [sensitivity to be stated: with the cross-stage dependence of execution time measured on AWS for our document pipeline (one document drives every stage), 1024 MB is not warm-feasible at 14.5 s either; both move to 2048 MB, both meet the target, and PRISM costs at least 17% more (development traces, measured per-size scaling, exploratory)]. Your Table S14 point also stands: fixed 2048 MB headroom was 14-29% cheaper than PRISM's best setting in the short-slack regimes (two traces, post hoc, no caps; 13-27% with the current code) [under the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, which the AWS measurements contradict above 2048 MB; to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling]. (4) Prespecified closed-loop comparison (C3: event-level simulator, 12 s, 25 traces, pre-calibration). PRISM cost 13.06% more than the memory-adaptive rule [95% CI +8.50, +17.63] and violated 0.655 points fewer windows [−0.921, −0.389]; PRISM met the 2% target in 24 of 25 weeks, the rule in 17. Because compliance differs, this cost difference is confounded and supports no cost ranking. (5) [Include only if the exploratory R2.7 test is reported.] Separately, an exploratory test with a step increase in execution time found no benefit of dynamic memory at the prespecified 50% increase: re-planning chose 2048 MB, which could not meet the target after the shift, while a one-time 3008 MB choice met it at 30-32% higher cost [under the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, which the AWS measurements contradict above 2048 MB; to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling]. It is not part of the RQ4 claim. (6) Changes for the manuscript pass (drafted, not yet applied): Section I and the RQ4 row of Table XIII: the narrowed RQ4, 'With one memory size shared by all stages and chosen hourly, and with execution characteristics fixed within each run, can moving to a larger memory size restore deadline compliance when the default size cannot meet it, and at what cost?'; the memory results (Section VI-G, Table IX, Table S14) and the third conclusion: the answer above, stated at identical compliance only; Section VIII: the limitation (per-stage memory and changes in execution characteristics within a run not tested); the graphical-abstract caption: the narrowed clause given in our reply to R5.m5(c); the abstract and contributions: no unscoped 'memory adaptation' claim. [Pending: manuscript pass; reporting result set for the 14-29% / 13-27% figure; whether the R2.7 test is reported.]

- Suggested response: Direct answer: we will name the workload in the Introduction: a synchronous, four-stage, synthetic document-ingestion pipeline with a configured end-to-end deadline of 14.5 s per record. (1) The pipeline. A client submits a document and waits while four sequential stages process it: ingest (normalize and tokenize), transform (redact e-mail addresses, phone numbers and ID numbers), aggregate (classify) and load (build a compressed inverted-index entry). The result must return within 14.5 s per record. It is a synthetic workload that we specified and implemented on AWS Lambda and Step Functions Express, not a production application (workload specification in the repository, aws_pipeline/SPEC.md). (2) Why provisioned concurrency matters here. Because the caller waits, Lambda initialization on any stage (a cold start) is spent inside that record's response-time budget. Provisioned concurrency keeps environments initialized in advance, but AWS notes that it does not come online immediately (Lambda starts allocating it after a minute or two of preparation) and that it runs continually and is billed separately. It therefore helps when demand is predictable enough to prepare environments before arrivals, which is the setting PRISM addresses. (3) Your point about AWS's guidance. We agree. AWS states that 'Generally, interactive workloads benefit the most from the feature' and that 'Asynchronous workloads, such as data processing pipelines, are often less latency sensitive and so do not usually need provisioned concurrency'. Our pipeline is the synchronous, interactive case; asynchronous pipelines without per-record deadlines are outside the paper's scope, and the text will say so. (4) Supporting measurements, kept separate (small-scale): (a) one end-to-end integration run on Lambda and Step Functions Express with 40 records per arm: on demand at a fixed 1024 MB (the baseline arm), 39 of 40 records met 14.5 s; under a fixed memory schedule that included 512 MB, 29 of 40 did, with most misses at 512 MB; this checked deployment, orchestration and deadline accounting, not PRISM; (b) direct invocations of each stage at four memory sizes measured service and initialization times (20,992 invocations); (c) a live comparison of PRISM's adaptive controller with a baseline on identical records has not been run, and we make no claim from it. (5) Changes for the manuscript pass (drafted, not yet applied): Section I: the use-case paragraph (proposal M7), citing both AWS Lambda Developer Guide pages ('Configuring provisioned concurrency for an Event Function' and 'Understanding Lambda Event Function scaling', accessed 23 Nov 2026); References: both pages; Section V or VIII: the measurement sentence above, with the arm named. [Pending: manuscript pass.]

## R4.8 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) Remove the about 3% of 1024 MB initialisations above the 10 s limit: resolved in code (truncation with a gate), not enabled. Correction (24 Nov 2026): AWS documents the 10 s Init limit as a timeout for on-demand functions that is retried at the first invocation, and not as a limit for provisioned concurrency; it is not a cap on start-up time, so the truncation would misrepresent long start-ups. The handling is part of decision P3.
- Changed / rationale: PipelineConfig.init_max_s truncates the log-normal at the cap with common random numbers kept (a0a7916). Default None until the measured init distribution is fitted, since the fit may replace the log-normal and the switch moves every risk table; enabling it earlier would mean regenerating twice.
- Evidence: Gate: no draw above 10 s at 512-3008 MB; closed-form truncated mean and CDF; cited shares 3.20% (1024 MB) and 16.40% (512 MB) under the submitted model (tests/test_analytic.py::TestTruncatedInit; NA-3). With the cap rho(1024) 0.531 -> 0.515. 22 Nov 2026 (billing and usage audit): measured platform initialisation, memcal-1: 4,992 forced cold starts at 512-3008 MB, maximum Init Duration 358.61 ms, none above 1 s (recomputed from the private run's REPORT records; p95 at most 136.6 ms per cell in prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/tables/timings.csv). This is for functions without application start-up work. 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): calibration-input audit (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_inputs_20261124/README.md): each PipelineConfig() constant mapped to the saved AWS evidence. warm_mean_1024 matches memcal-1 by construction (frozen work units), so it is not independent evidence; the Eq. (2) memory scaling is contradicted in form (no speed-up above 2048 MB; held-out fit fails at 16 of 16 cells); exec_shape 0.30 is untestable (one document; measured run-to-run sigma_log about 0.01-0.13); Lambda Init Duration (platform INIT phase, 0.115 s, flat) is distinguished from the client first-call penalty (0.23-0.51 s) and from application start-up (the modelled 6 s; not measured, no start-up work); stage-to-stage overhead measured at 23-27 ms against the assumed 0.1 s; activation lag, keep-alive and overflow unmeasured. No constant changed. 24 Nov 2026 (calibration pass, no AWS call, no rerun): correction: the reply said 'measured distribution'; the start-up distribution is declared (P3), not measured (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_20261124/CALIBRATION_TABLE.md). init_max_s = 10 s is supported by Lambda's documented INIT limit. 24 Nov 2026 (calibration decisions and bounded closed-loop check; no AWS call): AWS Lambda execution-environment documentation (lambda-runtime-environment.html, read 24 Nov 2026): 'The Init phase is limited to 10 seconds. If all three tasks do not complete within 10 seconds, Lambda retries the Init phase at the time of the first function invocation with the configured function timeout'; 'The 10-second timeout doesn't apply to functions that are using provisioned concurrency, SnapStart, or Lambda Managed Instances'. This corrects the statement of the calibration pass earlier today that init_max_s = 10 s is supported by the documented limit (dated notes in prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_20261124/). Under the declared 6 s, sigma 0.30 start-up, about 3.2% of draws exceed 10 s.
- Remaining: Blocker: AWS init fit. Next: set init_max_s = 10.0 (or the measured form) at the regeneration. Pass: P(init > 10 s) = 0 in the configuration that produces the reported results. Quota 10 measurement plan (8786295, proposal G; b9d84bd). Cold initialization vs memory is feasible. This is a measurement plan. 22 Nov 2026 (billing and usage audit): status unchanged. The 'AWS init fit' blocker is partly lifted: platform initialisation is measured and far below 10 s. The application start-up distribution (the modelled 6 s mean) is not measured, so P3 decides what replaces the log-normal. init_max_s = 10.0 is still to be enabled at the regeneration. 24 Nov 2026 (local technical pass, no AWS call): a calibrated run needs, in order: decision P3 (the application's start-up work: init_mean_1024, init_shape, init_scaling, then init_max_s); a per-size execution-time mode replacing Eq. (2) (a src/ change, deferred because src/ is pinned for the R6.1 run); P4 (input sizes: exec_shape); activation lag and overflow from the R6.1 live run. 24 Nov 2026 (calibration pass, no AWS call, no rerun): correction: the input-size decision is SPEC.md P2 (P4 is the source and sink); earlier notes said P4. Before the calibrated rerun (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_20261124/DECISION_RECORD.md section 5): author decisions P1, P2 (recommended: adopt log-normal length, mean 3,000 words, sigma 0.30, giving exec_shape 0.30 by construction), P3 (recommended: keep the 6 s as a declared start-up, constant in memory, init_max_s 10, with 3 s, CPU-bound and platform-only sensitivities), D-CAL-1 (adopt the per-size execution factors), P9, R2.11, D3; a frozen calibrated build after the R6.1 run; activation lag from the R6.1 run; burst overflow needs its own AWS test (the R6.1 run sheet does not measure it). 24 Nov 2026 (calibration decisions and bounded closed-loop check; no AWS call): decision P3 (prism-repro/docs/revision/calibration_final_20261124/DECISIONS.md section 3): recommended (c) declare the application's start-up below 10 s, with the untruncated model as a sensitivity; alternative (b) model timeout-and-retry. Do not enable init_max_s as 'the limit'.
- Suggested response: Thank you for checking this. AWS documents the 10 s limit differently from a cap: for on-demand functions, an initialisation that does not finish within 10 s is retried at the first invocation under the function timeout, and the limit does not apply to provisioned concurrency. A start-up longer than 10 s therefore makes that request slower rather than being cut short, so truncating the distribution at 10 s would misstate it. The 6 s mean start-up in our model is a declared assumption, not a measurement; our AWS campaign measured only Lambda's platform initialisation (about 0.115 s at every memory size) for functions that do no start-up work, and about 3% of the modelled draws exceed 10 s. We will state that the modelled application starts within 10 s and report the untruncated model as a sensitivity [pending decision P3].

Under the new spike, PRISM cost 1,595 USD per week with 1.67% violating windows; the calibrated q90 rule 1,653 USD and 1.67% (cost difference -3.2 +/- 5.5%, not distinguishable); reactive provisioning 2,165 USD and 1.76% (PRISM 26.3 +/- 3.1% cheaper on every trace); and smoothed headroom (PredHead+S) 1,415 USD and 3.96% (PRISM 13.1 +/- 8.0% costlier on every trace, with fewer violating windows on every trace, though that paired interval, -2.3 +/- 3.1 points, includes zero). These reproduce the ordering of the fast-simulator results without relying on its matched load model, and they are reported with cost and compliance together. The prespecified closed-loop comparisons on 25 further traces (C1-C3) agree where they overlap. [pre-calibration figures above; final values pending] The predictive advantage survives longer lags in our simulations, the recalibration grows more conservative on its own, and memory co-optimisation still switches to larger memory when initialisation does not shorten with memory, at 3-5% higher cost. On AWS Lambda, for our synthetic four-stage deployment whose functions do no application start-up work, Lambda's reported initialisation was about 115 ms at every memory size (median 113.7-116.0 ms). This is a measurement of the platform's initialisation for that deployment; it is not comparable with the 6 s modelled initialisation, which represents application start-up. Fitted on 29 of every 39 measurement visits and tested on the other 10, chosen in advance, 37 of 56 held-out predictions fell inside the held-out data's 95% batch-bootstrap interval (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/RESULTS.md). How the simulator should represent application start-up, and therefore where the initialisation sweep is centred, remains open.

The earlier fast-only floor comparison cannot resolve this request. 24 Nov 2026 (closed-loop follow-up pass, no AWS call): status unchanged (Partially fixed): the hold on B3 is lifted; apply B3 with the M20 sentence and (b) the conclusions text (P-A1, P-B1).
- Suggested response: We accept the analysis. Infeasible candidates are now ranked by their violation quantile, and the conclusions state that the chance constraint acted on capacity as a tuned quantile rule. On the floor: in the fast simulator, with eps retuned, lowering it made the estimate active but raised cost at equal compliance. Under closed-loop event-level evaluation the estimate is not at its floor at the selected budgets (mean about 0.42; at the floor in about 0.2% of test epochs), so the floor does not exclude the no-provisioning option there; lowering it changes almost no decision, and after retuning neither a lower floor nor a frozen estimate changes cost distinguishably (five development traces, exploratory, pre-calibration).
