The redrawn image uses the article title exactly: 'Joint Cost-SLA Provisioning of Concurrency and Memory for Serverless Data Pipelines: A Chance-Constrained Formulation and a Simulation Study under Workload Shift'. [Final check: confirm the title in the submission system for the revised submission.] (c) Narrow the memory claim. The caption's 'memory adaptation helping satisfy tighter latency targets' will be replaced by a clause matching the third conclusion, with the same scope as RQ4 (see R4.4): one memory size shared by all four stages, re-decided hourly (every 12 five-minute decision epochs; provisioned concurrency is decided every epoch), with execution characteristics (execution time, CPU intensity, memory demand, initialization latency) held fixed within each run. What the short-slack results support: in the tested regimes where 1024 MB was inadequate, PRISM selected a larger shared memory size when its risk budget was small enough (2048 MB or more in every epoch at ε = 0.02); and a fixed 2048 MB headroom rule met the 2% target at 13-27% lower cost than PRISM's best setting (1 − headroom cost / PRISM cost; mean over two traces; both settings chosen post hoc with test-week knowledge; no capacity caps; 27.21% at 13 s, 22.36% at 12 s and 13.03% at 11 s with non-scaling fraction 0.5, 13.66% at 12 s with 0.15; at 11 s the headroom rule violated 1.86% of windows against PRISM's 1.19%); the submitted Table S14 gives 14-29%. These results come from two traces with post hoc settings and fixed execution characteristics; they imply no general cost advantage for PRISM and no validated adaptation to changes within a run. Proposed caption clause: 'When the default 1024 MB cannot meet the deadline, PRISM selects a larger memory size if its risk budget is small enough; a fixed 2048 MB headroom rule met the 2% target at 13-27% lower cost than PRISM's best setting in the regimes tested (two traces, post hoc, no caps), and adaptation to changes in execution characteristics within a run was not tested.' (The range and the headline values follow the reporting result set; the 13-27% range also rests on the assumed Eq. (2) memory scaling, to be recomputed with the measured per-size scaling; the last clause changes if the exploratory R2.7 test is reported.) Changes for the manuscript pass (pending): replace the image and caption with the regenerated draft. [Pending: reporting result set; title check; insertion.]

- Suggested response: We deployed all four stages of the pipeline on AWS Lambda, orchestrated by AWS Step Functions (Express), and ran them end to end on AWS on a proposed synthetic document-ingestion workload, not a production application. Both arms processed the same 40 records against the 14.5 s per-record deadline. With memory fixed at 1024 MB, 39 of 40 records met it; under a fixed memory schedule (1024, 512, then 2048 MB), 29 of 40 did. No execution failed in either arm (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_live_integration/RESULTS.md; tables/arms.csv, tables/paired_deadlines.csv). This is a live integration test of the pipeline and of the interface the controller uses, driven by a fixed schedule. PRISM's adaptive controller has not yet been validated live. We have prepared that comparison: PRISM's own controller and an on-demand baseline replay the same 323 records of a thinned trace week through the same deployment, with PRISM's provisioned concurrency applied through published aliases and its readiness, activation lag and spill-over to on-demand instances recorded. It is verified against emulated AWS clients and awaits execution on AWS. Until it runs, the paper's compliance and cost results remain simulation-based; we state both as limitations. [Pending: live run.]

Its own teardown again reports NOT CLEAN with only the Express state machine listed (asynchronous deletion); no later listing is saved. That re-run rewrote the working copy of livetest.json; the archived copies match the collector package's sources.sha256. 22 Nov 2026 cleanup timeline (status unchanged): (1) 09:08 CST, run 090236 teardown: NOT CLEAN, Express state machine listed (asynchronous deletion). (2) 12:56:22 CST, saved preflight of the live-test re-run: no prism-pipe- leftovers, so the 09:08 state machine had been deleted. (3) 12:57:10 CST, that re-run's teardown: NOT CLEAN, Express state machine listed (saved in livetest.log). (4) Later outcome: a subsequent read-only verification in us-east-2 returned CLEAN, with no remaining prism-pipe- functions, state machines, log groups or roles. Source: reported by the author from the terminal; the verify step writes no file, so its output is not in the saved run files. This cleanup check answers no reviewer request and changes no status. 23 Nov 2026 (R6.1 replay arm, local only, no AWS call): the R6.1 live check can now measure activation lag per provisioned-concurrency change (10-s polling) and spill-over by initialisation type (prism-repro/docs/revision/r61_live_plan_20261123/PLAN.md section 5); nothing has been measured. Controlled burst overflow needs a separate step-load test; billing against the invoice stays with R2.14. - Suggested response: On AWS Lambda we measured cold starts, service times, billing fields and on-demand concurrency. For the four stage functions of a synthetic document pipeline, invoked individually at 512, 1024, 2048 and 3008 MB, we recorded 20,992 invocations in three blocks repeated on one afternoon, every one matched to Lambda's REPORT record, with up to eight invocations in flight and no throttling or failure. All 4,992 forced cold starts, and 128 more in a smoke test, reported an initialisation, with a median of 113.7-116.0 ms per stage and size (5th-95th percentiles 94.7-136.6 ms) for functions without start-up work; no warm invocation reported one. Median warm service times per stage ranged from 1.6-8.0 s at 512 MB to 0.46-2.29 s at 2048 and 3008 MB. Billed duration equalled the reported duration plus initialisation, rounded up to 1 ms, in 21,154 of 21,248 invocations, and was 1 ms higher in the other 94, all at whole-millisecond sums (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_memcal_20261122/RESULTS.md). In an earlier end-to-end run of the pipeline on Lambda and Step Functions Express at a fixed 1024 MB, the median end-to-end time was 9.08 s and the 95th percentile 12.33 s, with up to eight pipelines in flight and no execution failure (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_live_integration/RESULTS.md). Provisioned-concurrency activation lag and overflow were not measured, and billing is reconciled only provisionally: AWS's estimated usage export for the region and day contains the measured campaign usage (prism-repro/docs/revision/billing_reconciliation_20261124/reconciliation.csv), but final charges are not yet available (see our response to Reviewer 2, comment 14); these remain open, and the paper's activation parameters remain modelled values. [Manuscript section and table: pending.]

## R6.7 | Partially fixed
- Requests: (a) Explain the optimizer's practical value when it rarely chose above the smallest feasible capacity: resolved as an evidenced answer, now with its eps range and end-to-end evidence; manuscript text open.
- Changed / rationale: Shares R2.5, R4.1, R7.1 (066f69c).
- Evidence: Above the smallest feasible level in 16 of 40,293 feasible epochs (submitted code) and at most 0.11% for current code at eps up to 0.05; not ties (each such choice saves 1e-5 to 6e-3 of the epoch cost). At eps 0.08 23.7%, at 0.10-0.20 72-100%; end to end the cost against QuantProv-q90 then stays within -1.2 +/- 2.2% (wider grid) or turns against PRISM (+11.1 +/- 0.8% above break-even). Static restriction cost 2.71%; end-to-end integer-search saving 1.92% with worse compliance in 4 of 6 runs (NA-4, NA-5).
- Remaining: Blocker: none. Next: P-A1 to P-A4 with the corrected wording. Pass: text applied.
- Suggested response: Under the evaluated conditions the optimizer's practical value is small. Where it does choose above the smallest feasible level, at larger risk budgets, under stage-specific demand and above break-even, the end-to-end cost is no better than a calibrated quantile rule with the same information, and above break-even it is worse. We state this, with the conditions tested.

Each factor alone therefore removes more than the whole fast-to-event gap (162% and 154% of it), so in the simulator the excess comes from cold starts. The two factors are not additive shares: both act through cold starts. Consistent with this, on identical schedules the fast simulator records 3.23x fewer cold starts per on-demand request (a10efe6, prism-repro/docs/revision/kappa_gap/). Which aspect of initialisation matters (environments occupied while initialising, or the latency added to a request) and the downstream-dependency mechanism were not separated. The n = 1 line above is superseded. 23 Nov 2026 (label fix and Linux rebuild): Table X with the fixed code: PRISM 1.98% fast and 6.99% event level on Linux, 1.69% and 6.14% on Windows (stored 1.44% and 3.75%); Static and Reactive identical on both platforms (prism-repro/docs/revision/label_fix_20261123/README.md section 4).

(b) still needs the closed loop and measured constants. 22 Nov 2026 (resumed pass): (a) answered in simulation at the two-factor level. Optional: separate initialisation occupancy from initialisation latency by a further counterfactual. (b) needs the measured initialisation and lag (AWS campaigns; D7) and the closed-loop, calibrated evaluation. The live fixed-schedule run measured only about 0.23 s of platform start-up for handlers with no start-up work, against the assumed 6 s mean initialisation, so the size of the real-world effect depends on the stages' start-up work (decision P3). Pass: (b) stated from measured constants. 23 Nov 2026 (final audit): clarification of the 0.23 s above: it is the client-visible added latency of a cold stage in the live fixed-schedule run (prism-repro/docs/revision/aws_live_followup/README.md), not a Lambda Init Duration (memcal-1: about 115 ms) and not the modelled 6 s application start-up. Also, the stored Table X figures (PRISM 1.44% fast, 3.75% event) are SUBMITTED; the regenerated Table X at fd8b01a (E9, run through the label-lookup shim; fast simulator against event level on the same schedules, pre-calibration; prism-repro/docs/revision/regeneration_20261122/outputs/tables/T10_fast_vs_event.md) gives 1.69% and 6.14%; the decomposition was run on the stored schedules and must be redone on the regenerated ones after the E9 fix. 23 Nov 2026 (evidence review): Table X now rebuilds from the fixed release code (uncommitted; identical to the shim outputs on Windows); the decomposition still has to be redone on the regenerated schedules of the reporting platform. 23 Nov 2026 (commit 6e0f05c and reproduction package): the E9 fix referred to above is committed (6e0f05c).
- Suggested response: In our simulations the higher event-level violation rate comes from how cold starts are modelled. We replayed the schedules of Table X at event level with each modelling factor removed: with initialisation made instantaneous, PRISM's violating windows fall from 3.75% to 0.00%; with idle environments kept alive indefinitely, to 0.19%; the fast simulator gives 1.44% [figures to be regenerated]. Either change removes the whole gap, so both act through cold starts, which the fast simulator undercounts about threefold on the same schedules. How large the effect is on a real platform depends on the initialisation time of the deployed functions; we report it from measurements [pending] and do not yet claim it.

- Suggested response: The relation is empirical: the weekly violating-window rate rises monotonically with eps, and a prediction interval fitted on validation weeks covered [pending] of held-out weeks. Because eps bounds a per-record probability, it must sit below the 1% per-window allowance when warm misses dominate, which we now state.

- Suggested response: We accept this. Every comparison now carries its interval and a classification by rules fixed before the main runs: a difference only when the 95% interval excludes zero, a tie only when it lies within +/-2%, and cost compared only at similar compliance (within +/-0.5 points). In the prespecified main comparison (25 traces; pre-calibration, run on Windows), PRISM and the calibrated q90 rule are not distinguishable in cost (+0.30% [-1.42, +2.01]), and they are not tied either. In the post hoc matched-compliance analysis, we used test-week results from five development traces in the fast simulator with assumed constants. The interpolated paired cost interval excluded zero at the 1.5% target on Linux (-1.8% [-3.3%, -0.3%]) and at the 1.0% target on Windows (-2.3% [-4.3%, -0.3%]), both on all five traces. In both cases, the endpoint closest to zero was within 0.3 percentage points of zero, and the target at which the interval excluded zero varied by platform. No regenerated interval fell entirely within +/-2%, the tie margin prespecified for C1-C3 and used here as a benchmark. These results therefore provide only limited, platform- and target-specific evidence of lower cost: they do not establish a cost advantage that is consistent across targets or platforms, and they leave equivalence unestablished. The estimates at the 2.0% target on both platforms, and at the 1.5% target on Windows, used four traces, because PRISM's frontier did not bracket the target on trace 105. [Values pending the reporting platform and the rerun on measured constants.]
